Q31. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2024 (2 Marks)
What is meant by anomers? Draw the Haworth projection structures of α-D-glucopyranose and β-D-glucopyranose.
Answer: Definition of anomers and Haworth structures of glucose
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
1. Anomers: Diastereomeric monosaccharides that differ in stereochemical configuration exclusively at the hemiacetal or hemiketal carbon atom (C-1 in aldoses, C-2 in ketoses), called the anomeric carbon. 2. Haworth Projections: - α-D-glucopyranose: Pyranose ring with the anomeric -OH group at C-1 pointing downwards (trans to the -CH2OH group at C-5). - β-D-glucopyranose: Pyranose ring with the anomeric -OH group at C-1 pointing upwards (cis to the -CH2OH group at C-5).
Q32. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2023 (3 Marks)
Give chemical reactions to prove the following facts about the structure of D-glucose: (i) Presence of an aldehyde group (ii) Presence of five hydroxyl (-OH) groups (iii) Straight chain of six carbon atoms.
Answer: Three structural proofs of D-glucose
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Presence of Aldehyde Group: Glucose on oxidation with mild bromine water (Br2/H2O) forms gluconic acid, confirming the presence of an aldehyde (-CHO) group: CHO-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + [O] -(Br2/H2O)-> COOH-(CHOH)4-CH2OH (Gluconic acid). (ii) Presence of Five Hydroxyl Groups: Acetylation of glucose with acetic anhydride in the presence of pyridine yields glucose pentaacetate, confirming the presence of five -OH groups: C6H12O6 + 5 (CH3CO)2O -> Glucose pentaacetate + 5 CH3COOH. (iii) Straight Chain of Six Carbons: Prolonged heating of glucose with concentrated hydriodic acid (HI) and red phosphorus yields n-hexane, proving that all six carbons are linked in an unbranched linear chain: C6H12O6 + 14 HI -(heat / red P)-> CH3-CH2-CH2-CH2-CH2-CH3 (n-hexane) + 6 H2O + 7 I2.
Q33. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2023 (2 Marks)
Differentiate between fibrous proteins and globular proteins (any two points).
Answer: Differences between fibrous and globular proteins
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
1. Molecular Shape: Fibrous proteins have polypeptide chains running parallel along a single axis to form long, fiber-like thread structures. Globular proteins have polypeptide chains folded tightly into compact, spherical, three-dimensional shapes. 2. Solubility in Water: Fibrous proteins are insoluble in water (due to strong intermolecular hydrogen bonds and hydrophobic interactions). Globular proteins are soluble in water (hydrophilic groups face outward). 3. Examples: Fibrous = Keratin (hair/nails), Collagen (tendons), Myosin (muscles). Globular = Insulin, Hemoglobin, Albumin.
Q34. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2022 (3 Marks)
Define the following terms with one example each: (i) Peptide linkage (ii) Invert sugar (iii) Zwitterion.
Answer: Definitions and examples
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Peptide Linkage: A covalent amide bond (-CO-NH-) formed between the carboxyl group (-COOH) of one α-amino acid and the amino group (-NH2) of another α-amino acid with elimination of water. Example: Glycylalanine (Gly-Ala). (ii) Invert Sugar: The equimolar mixture of D-(+)-glucose and D-(-)-fructose obtained by acid or enzymatic hydrolysis of sucrose, having a net laevorotatory optical rotation (-39.9°) opposite to the original dextrorotation (+66.5°) of sucrose. (iii) Zwitterion: A dipolar, electrically neutral ion carrying equal and opposite formal electrical charges (+NH3 and -COO⁻) in the same molecule. Example: +H3N-CH2-COO⁻ (glycine zwitterion in water).
Q35. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2020 (2 Marks)
What is denaturation of proteins? What happens to the native structure and biological activity during denaturation?
Answer: Denaturation concept and structural effects
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
1. Definition: The phenomenon where a native protein is exposed to physical changes (such as heat) or chemical changes (such as pH change, heavy metal ions), causing disruption of hydrogen bonds and electrostatic interactions. 2. Effects on Structure and Activity: The compact globular tertiary and helical secondary structures unfold into random coils, destroying the active site geometry. The protein coagulates and completely loses its biological activity. Importantly, the primary structure (covalent peptide backbone) remains unaffected.
Q36. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2019 (3 Marks)
State the deficiency disease caused by lack of each of the following vitamins: (i) Vitamin A (ii) Vitamin B1 (Thiamine) (iii) Vitamin D.
Answer: Deficiency diseases of vitamins A, B1, and D
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Vitamin A (Retinol): Night blindness (nyctalopia) and Xerophthalmia (hardening/keratinization of the cornea of the eye). (ii) Vitamin B1 (Thiamine): Beriberi (characterized by peripheral neuropathy, muscle wasting, and cardiac failure). (iii) Vitamin D (Calciferol): Rickets in growing children (bow legs, bone deformities) and Osteomalacia in adults (soft, brittle bones).
Q37. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2018 (2 Marks)
Differentiate between DNA and RNA with respect to: (i) Pentose sugar present (ii) Nitrogenous pyrimidine bases present.
Answer: Chemical differences between DNA and RNA
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Pentose Sugar: DNA contains β-D-2-deoxyribose (lacks oxygen at C-2' position). RNA contains β-D-ribose (possesses a hydroxyl group -OH at C-2' position). (ii) Pyrimidine Bases: DNA contains Cytosine (C) and Thymine (T). RNA contains Cytosine (C) and Uracil (U) instead of thymine.
Q38. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2017 (3 Marks)
Explain why: (i) Cellulose cannot be used as a source of energy for humans. (ii) Amino acids have high melting points and are soluble in water unlike haloacids. (iii) Vitamin C cannot be stored in our body.
Answer: Explanations for cellulose digestion, amino acid physical properties, and vitamin C excretion
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Cellulose Digestion: Cellulose contains β-(1->4) glycosidic bonds. Human digestive enzymes (like salivary and pancreatic amylases) hydrolyze only α-glycosidic bonds. Humans lack the enzyme cellulase needed to cleave β-linkages. (ii) Amino Acid Properties: Amino acids exist in the solid state as dipolar zwitterions (+H3N-CHR-COO⁻). Strong electrostatic ionic attractions between oppositely charged zwitterionic ends require immense thermal energy to break (high melting points >250 °C). Furthermore, the ionic charges form strong ion-dipole interactions and hydrogen bonds with water molecules, making them readily water-soluble. (iii) Vitamin C Storage: Vitamin C (ascorbic acid) is a water-soluble vitamin. Excess amounts absorbed are readily filtered by the kidneys and excreted in urine, so it cannot be stored in the body.
Q39. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2016 (2 Marks)
Write the structural differences between starch and cellulose.
Answer: Structural comparison of starch and cellulose
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
1. Monomeric Unit: Starch is a polymer of α-D-glucose; Cellulose is a linear polymer of β-D-glucose. 2. Linkages and Branching: Starch consists of amylose (linear α-1,4-linkages) and amylopectin (branched, with α-1,4-linear and α-1,6-branch linkages). Cellulose consists exclusively of unbranched, linear chains held together by β-1,4-glycosidic linkages that pack tightly into rigid fibrous bundles via extensive interchain hydrogen bonds.
Q40. Short Answer (2-3M)
CBSE 2015 (2 Marks)
What are essential and non-essential amino acids? Give one example of each.
Answer: Essential vs non-essential amino acids
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
1. Essential Amino Acids: Amino acids that cannot be synthesized by the human body and must be supplied through regular dietary sources. Example: Valine, Leucine, Lysine. 2. Non-Essential Amino Acids: Amino acids that can be synthesized by the human body from metabolic precursors and do not strictly require dietary intake. Example: Glycine, Alanine, Glutamic acid.
Q41. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2024 (5 Marks)
(a) Discuss the primary, secondary, tertiary, and quaternary structures of proteins. (b) What type of bonding stabilizes the α-helix and β-pleated sheet structures? (c) Name one example each of a fibrous protein and a globular protein.
Answer: Four levels of protein structure, secondary bonding, and examples
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) Four Levels of Protein Architecture (3 marks): 1. Primary Structure: The linear, specific sequence in which various α-amino acids are linked by covalent peptide bonds in a polypeptide chain. Any alteration in this sequence completely disrupts protein function (e.g. Sickle-cell anaemia). 2. Secondary Structure: The regular local conformation of the polypeptide backbone resulting from hydrogen bonding between the >C=O and -NH- groups of peptide bonds. Main types: α-helix and β-pleated sheet. 3. Tertiary Structure: The overall three-dimensional folding of the entire polypeptide chain, producing complex compact shapes. Stabilized by disulfide bonds (-S-S-), hydrogen bonds, ionic/electrostatic bonds, hydrophobic interactions, and van der Waals forces. Determines fibrous vs globular character. 4. Quaternary Structure: The spatial arrangement and association of two or more independent folded polypeptide subunits into a single multi-subunit oligomeric protein complex (e.g. Hemoglobin with 2α and 2β subunits). (b) Secondary Structure Stabilization (1 mark): - α-Helix: Stabilized by intramolecular hydrogen bonds between the -NH- of one amino acid and the >C=O of the fourth residue ahead. - β-Pleated Sheet: Stabilized by intermolecular hydrogen bonds between >C=O and -NH- groups of adjacent parallel or antiparallel polypeptide strands. (c) Examples (1 mark): - Fibrous protein: Keratin (hair/nails) or Collagen (connective tissue). - Globular protein: Insulin (pancreatic hormone) or Hemoglobin (oxygen transport).
Q42. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2023 (5 Marks)
(a) Write the chemical reactions of D-glucose with: (i) Hydroxylamine (NH2OH) (ii) Hydrogen cyanide (HCN) (iii) Acetic anhydride ((CH3CO)2O) (iv) Concentrated nitric acid (conc. HNO3) (v) Bromine water (Br2 / H2O). (b) State two experimental observations that could not be explained by the open-chain structure of D-glucose.
Answer: Five diagnostic reactions of glucose and limitations of open-chain structure
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) Chemical Reactions of D-Glucose (3.5 marks): (i) With NH2OH: Forms glucose oxime: CHO-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + H2N-OH -> CH=N-OH-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + H2O. (ii) With HCN: Forms glucose cyanohydrin: CHO-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + HCN -> CH(OH)(CN)-(CHOH)4-CH2OH. (iii) With Acetic Anhydride: Forms glucose pentaacetate: C6H12O6 + 5 (CH3CO)2O -(pyridine)-> Glucose pentaacetate + 5 CH3COOH. (iv) With conc. HNO3: Forms Saccharic acid: CHO-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + 3 [O] -(conc. HNO3)-> COOH-(CHOH)4-COOH + 2 H2O. (v) With Br2 water: Forms Gluconic acid: CHO-(CHOH)4-CH2OH + [O] -(Br2/H2O)-> COOH-(CHOH)4-CH2OH. (b) Limitations of Open-Chain Structure (1.5 marks): 1. Glucose does not give Schiff's test, nor does it form a sodium hydrogen sulfite (NaHSO3) addition compound, indicating absence of a free -CHO group. 2. Glucose pentaacetate does not react with hydroxylamine (NH2OH), proving that the anomeric C-1 is locked in a cyclic hemiacetal ring. 3. Glucose exists in two distinct crystalline anomeric forms: α-D-glucose (mp 419 K, [α]D = +112°) and β-D-glucose (mp 423 K, [α]D = +19°), which undergo mutarotation to an equilibrium mixture ([α]D = +52.5°).
Q43. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2022 (5 Marks)
(a) Describe the double helix model of DNA proposed by Watson and Crick. (b) What are Chargaff's rules? (c) A segment of a DNA strand has the base sequence: 5'-A-T-G-C-C-T-A-3'. Write the base sequence of the complementary strand.
Answer: Watson-Crick DNA model, Chargaff's rules, and complementary base sequence
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) Watson-Crick DNA Model (2.5 marks): 1. DNA consists of two polynucleotide strands coiled around a central axis to form a right-handed double helix. 2. The two strands run in opposite, antiparallel directions: one runs in 5' -> 3' direction, while the other runs in 3' -> 5' direction. 3. The sugar-phosphate backbone forms the outer structural framework, while nitrogenous bases project inward into the helix perpendicular to the axis. 4. The two strands are held together by specific hydrogen bonds between purines and pyrimidines: - Adenine pairs with Thymine via two hydrogen bonds (A = T). - Guanine pairs with Cytosine via three hydrogen bonds (G ≡ C). 5. Pitch of the helix is 3.4 nm (34 Å), containing roughly 10 base pairs per turn (0.34 nm per base pair). (b) Chargaff's Rules (1.5 marks): In any double-stranded DNA molecule: 1. The molar ratio of Adenine to Thymine is strictly equal to 1: [A] = [T]. 2. The molar ratio of Guanine to Cytosine is strictly equal to 1: [G] = [C]. 3. Total purines equal total pyrimidines: [A + G] = [T + C]. (c) Complementary Base Sequence (1 mark): Given: 5'-A-T-G-C-C-T-A-3' Complementary strand: 3'-T-A-C-G-G-A-T-5' (or 5'-T-A-G-G-C-A-T-3').
Q44. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2021 (Case Study - 4 Marks)
Read the following passage and answer the questions: Case Study: Carbohydrate Chemistry in Nutrition and Disease Carbohydrates serve as vital metabolic fuels and structural components in all living systems. Monosaccharides undergo intramolecular cyclization to form cyclic hemiacetals (pyranose and furanose rings). Disaccharides like sucrose, maltose, and lactose are formed by condensation of two monosaccharides with elimination of water, linked via glycosidic bonds. Polysaccharides function as energy reserves (starch in plants, glycogen in animals) or structural elements (cellulose in plants). In diabetics, elevated glucose levels can be monitored using glucose oxidase test strips or clinical Benedict's reagent. (i) Name the two monosaccharide units produced upon hydrolysis of lactose. (ii) Why is maltose called a reducing sugar? (iii) What structural difference distinguishes amylose from amylopectin in starch? (iv) Which polysaccharide is stored in the human liver as an emergency glucose reserve?
Answer: Solutions to Case Study on Carbohydrates in Nutrition and Biology
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) β-D-galactose and β-D-glucose (linked via β-1,4-glycosidic bond). (ii) Maltose contains two α-D-glucose units linked by an α-1,4-glycosidic bond. The hemiacetal carbon (C-1) of the second glucose unit remains free and can open to form an aldehyde group that reduces Tollens' and Fehling's reagents. (iii) Amylose is a water-soluble, unbranched linear polymer of α-D-glucose linked strictly by α-(1->4) glycosidic bonds (15-20% of starch). Amylopectin is a water-insoluble, highly branched polymer containing α-(1->4) linear chains with α-(1->6) glycosidic branching points every 20-25 glucose units (80-85% of starch). (iv) Glycogen (animal starch).
Q45. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2020 (5 Marks)
(a) Classify vitamins into fat-soluble and water-soluble vitamins. Give two examples of each group. (b) Name the deficiency disease caused by lack of: (i) Vitamin B12 (ii) Vitamin E (iii) Vitamin K (iv) Vitamin B2 (Riboflavin). (c) Why should vitamin B-complex and vitamin C be taken daily in diet?
Answer: Vitamin classification, deficiency diseases, and dietary requirement
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) Classification of Vitamins (2 marks): 1. Fat-Soluble Vitamins: Soluble in fats and oils, insoluble in water. Stored in liver and adipose (fat-storing) tissues. Examples: Vitamin A, Vitamin D, Vitamin E, Vitamin K. 2. Water-Soluble Vitamins: Soluble in water, insoluble in fats. Examples: Vitamin B-complex (B1, B2, B6, B12) and Vitamin C (ascorbic acid). (b) Deficiency Diseases (2 marks): (i) Vitamin B12: Pernicious anaemia (severe reduction in RBC production and neurological damage). (ii) Vitamin E: Muscular dystrophy / weakness and increased fragility of RBCs / loss of reproductive fertility. (iii) Vitamin K: Defective blood clotting / delayed clotting time (hemorrhagic tendency). (iv) Vitamin B2 (Riboflavin): Cheilosis (fissuring at corners of mouth and lips), digestive disorders, and glossitis (inflamed tongue). (c) Dietary Requirement (1 mark): Water-soluble vitamins cannot be stored in the body in appreciable amounts (except B12) because they are continually excreted through urine; hence they must be regularly supplied in our daily diet.
Q46. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2019 (5 Marks)
(a) Differentiate between nucleoside and nucleotide. Draw schematic diagrams showing their general assembly. (b) What are the three structural components of a nucleotide in RNA? (c) State two fundamental biological functions of nucleic acids.
Answer: Nucleoside vs nucleotide, RNA components, and biological functions
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(a) Nucleoside vs Nucleotide (2.5 marks): 1. Nucleoside: Formed by attachment of a nitrogenous base (purine or pyrimidine) to the C-1' carbon of a pentose sugar via an N-glycosidic bond: Nucleoside = Pentose Sugar + Nitrogenous Base. 2. Nucleotide: Formed when the C-5' hydroxyl group of the pentose sugar in a nucleoside is esterified with phosphoric acid: Nucleotide = Pentose Sugar + Nitrogenous Base + Phosphate group. (Nucleotide = Nucleoside 5'-monophosphate). (b) Components in RNA (1 mark): 1. Pentose Sugar: β-D-Ribose. 2. Nitrogenous Bases: Adenine (A), Guanine (G), Cytosine (C), and Uracil (U). 3. Inorganic Acid: Phosphoric acid (H3PO4). (c) Biological Functions (1.5 marks): 1. Replication and Heredity: DNA stores genetic instructions and replicates itself during cell division, transmitting hereditary information faithfully from generation to generation. 2. Protein Biosynthesis: RNA molecules (mRNA, tRNA, rRNA) direct and synthesize all proteins and enzymes required for life according to genetic codons transcribe from DNA.
Q47. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2018 (4 Marks)
Account for the following observations: (i) Sucrose is dextrorotatory, but its hydrolysis product is laevorotatory. (ii) Amino acids behave like salts rather than simple amines or carboxylic acids. (iii) Helical structure of proteins is called secondary structure. (iv) Glucose and fructose both give the same osazone on reaction with excess phenylhydrazine.
Answer: Four fundamental chemical explanations in biomolecules
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(i) Invert Sugar: Sucrose has dextrorotation of +66.5°. Hydrolysis yields equal amounts of D-(+)-glucose (+52.5°) and D-(-)-fructose (-92.4°). The powerful laevorotation of fructose outweighs the dextrorotation of glucose, rendering the net hydrolysate mixture laevorotatory (-39.9°). (ii) Salt-like Nature: Amino acids contain both an acidic -COOH and a basic -NH2 group. Internal proton transfer forms a dipolar zwitterion (+H3N-CHR-COO⁻). Strong intermolecular electrostatic ionic attractions give amino acids crystalline solid forms, high melting points (>250 °C), and water solubility, characteristic of ionic salts. (iii) Secondary Structure Designation: 'Secondary' refers specifically to the local spatial conformational arrangement that the linear polypeptide backbone assumes (such as α-helix or β-sheet) through regular hydrogen bonding, preceding overall tertiary folding. (iv) Identical Osazone: In osazone formation with phenylhydrazine, reaction involves only C-1 and C-2 carbon atoms. Since D-glucose and D-fructose have identical stereochemical configurations at carbons C-3, C-4, and C-5, both monosaccharides yield the exact same glucosazone crystals.
Q48. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2017 (5 Marks)
(a) What are reducing and non-reducing sugars? Give one example of each. (b) Explain the glycosidic linkage present in: (i) Maltose (ii) Lactose (iii) Sucrose. (c) How will you show that glucose contains a primary alcoholic group?
Answer: Reducing vs non-reducing sugars, glycosidic linkages in disaccharides, and primary alcohol proof
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) Reducing vs Non-Reducing Sugars (1.5 marks): - Reducing Sugars: Carbohydrates containing a free or potential aldehyde or keto group (free hemiacetal/hemiketal OH at anomeric carbon) that reduce Tollens' and Fehling's solutions. Example: Glucose, Fructose, Maltose, Lactose. - Non-Reducing Sugars: Carbohydrates in which the anomeric carbons of all monosaccharide units are locked in glycosidic linkages, preventing ring opening. Example: Sucrose, Starch, Cellulose. (b) Glycosidic Linkages (2 marks): (i) Maltose: Formed between C-1 of one α-D-glucose unit and C-4 of another α-D-glucose unit (α-1,4-glycosidic linkage). (ii) Lactose: Formed between C-1 of β-D-galactose and C-4 of β-D-glucose (β-1,4-glycosidic linkage). (iii) Sucrose: Formed between C-1 of α-D-glucose and C-2 of β-D-fructose (α-1,β-2-glycosidic linkage). (c) Primary Alcohol Proof in Glucose (1.5 marks): Oxidation of glucose with concentrated nitric acid (conc. HNO3) yields the dicarboxylic acid Saccharic acid (HOOC-(CHOH)4-COOH). Since nitric acid oxidizes the terminal -CHO and primary alcohol -CH2OH to -COOH, this confirms the presence of one primary alcohol group at C-6.
Q49. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2016 (4 Marks)
(a) What are vitamins? How are they classified? (b) Which disease is caused by the deficiency of: (i) Thiamine (ii) Ascorbic acid (iii) Pyridoxine (iv) Calciferol?
Answer: Vitamins definition, classification, and four deficiency diseases
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
(a) Definition and Classification (2 marks): - Vitamins: Organic compounds required in minute amounts in the diet for specific biological functions, maintenance of optimum growth, and normal health of an organism. - Classification: 1. Fat-Soluble: Dissolve in fats/oils (Vitamins A, D, E, K). Stored in liver and fatty tissues. 2. Water-Soluble: Dissolve in water (Vitamins B-complex and C). Excreted in urine; cannot be stored (except B12). (b) Deficiency Diseases (2 marks): (i) Thiamine (Vitamin B1): Beriberi. (ii) Ascorbic Acid (Vitamin C): Scurvy. (iii) Pyridoxine (Vitamin B6): Convulsions, peripheral neuropathy. (iv) Calciferol (Vitamin D): Rickets (in children) and Osteomalacia (in adults).
Q50. Long Answer / Case Study (4-5M)
CBSE 2015 (5 Marks)
(a) What is the difference between an α-amino acid and a β-amino acid? (b) Write the zwitterionic structure of alanine. (c) What are the forces that stabilize the tertiary structure of proteins? (d) Name the purine bases and pyrimidine bases found in DNA.
Answer: Amino acid distinction, alanine zwitterion, tertiary stabilizing forces, and DNA bases
Step-by-Step Marking Scheme & Explanation:
Marking Scheme: (a) α vs β Amino Acid (1 mark): - In α-amino acids, the amino group (-NH2) is attached to the α-carbon (carbon directly adjacent to the -COOH group): R-CH(NH2)-COOH. - In β-amino acids, the amino group is attached to the β-carbon (two carbons away from -COOH): R-CH(NH2)-CH2-COOH. (b) Alanine Zwitterion (1 mark): +H3N-CH(CH3)-COO⁻. (c) Stabilizing Forces in Tertiary Structure (2 marks): 1. Disulfide linkages: Covalent -S-S- bonds between cysteine residues. 2. Hydrogen bonds: Between polar side-chain groups (-OH, -NH2, -COOH). 3. Electrostatic / Ionic bonds (Salt bridges): Between oppositely charged side chains (-NH3⁺ and -COO⁻). 4. Hydrophobic interactions: Association of non-polar hydrophobic side chains (leucine, isoleucine, phenylalanine) in the interior core away from water. 5. van der Waals forces: Weak dispersion forces between close-packed atoms. (d) Bases in DNA (1 mark): - Purines: Adenine (A) and Guanine (G). - Pyrimidines: Cytosine (C) and Thymine (T).
